A song carries the plugin's recording MBID and ISRC onto the played track (`recommendedSongTrack`: `Track.recordingMbid` / `isrc`, kept only when well-formed); `duration` is converted from seconds to ms. The resolver-loader's seven recommendation methods (`getRecommendationResolvers`, `getRecommendations`, `expandRecommendationSection`, `getHistoryMixResolvers`, `getHistoryMixes`, `getSimilarUsersResolvers`, `getSimilarUsers`) are copied from desktop's into both apps' copies, which a test keeps identical. A fix to a service's fetch belongs in its `.axe`, not here. **The screens.** Android's Recommendations page (`RecommendationsScreen`) draws the shared page: `RecommendationPageView.kt` holds its rules (which rows and sections show under the source filter, the previews, See all / Show less, the skeletons and where they go: `albumsSlotIndex`, `pendingSkeleton`, `listenersSkeleton`, the page's state: loading, not configured, failed, empty, content), `RecommendationPageLayout.kt` the blocks in drawing order (`recommendationPageLayout`: section titles, rows, the skeletons; Android draws it and iOS's `IosRecommendationsPage` bridge maps it) and `RecommendationCopy` its words, and `RecommendationQueues.kt` what a tap plays (a song and the rest of its row, an album, a weekly or service playlist, a listener's top tracks). **A recommended service playlist opens in its own view (desktop's `openServicePlaylist`)**: a tap on its card opens `ServicePlaylistScreen` (`Routes.servicePlaylist`), which looks the tracks up through the registry's link lookup (`servicePlaylistTracks`), shows "Recommended by <service>" with Play and Save, and saves nothing until Save, which imports the link like any playlist link (`PlaylistImportManager.importFromUrl`) under a key taken from the link (`recommendedPlaylistKey`, `PlaylistUrlImporter`'s `savedKey`), so saving it again on a later visit opens the first copy, and opens the saved playlist; its long-press menu keeps Play and Add to Queue. A card with no link plays instead (`playlistTapOpensView`). A queue played from it carries the `service-playlist` context (`SERVICE_PLAYLIST_CONTEXT`, id = the link), and Now Playing's "Playing from" opens the view again (the shared `playbackContextDestination`), not a saved playlist. Weekly cards' mosaics and tracks come from the shared `WeeklyCovers` and `weeklyTracks`. The page shows a filter chip per source, then Tracks, Albums, Artists, Playlists and Listeners; Not configured links to the ListenBrainz and Last.fm plugin sheets (`Routes.settings(pluginId)`, `settings?plugin=<id>`). iOS's page (`RecommendationsScreen.swift`) draws the same shared page, through the `IosRecommendationsPage.kt` bridge (flat DTOs of `recommendationPageLayout`'s blocks, watched through the screen's own `IosRecommendationsSession`, which holds its picked source and open rows as Android's view model does), with the same cards, See all grid (the shared `recommendationGridColumns`: as many cards as fit at `CARD_MIN_GRID_WIDTH`, at least 2), long-press menus and Connect links (`PCOpenPluginSettings` opens Settings on the plugin's sheet); a recommended service playlist's tap opens `ServicePlaylistView` (`PCRoute.servicePlaylist`), the same view as Android's with Play and a keyed Save (`IosContainer.saveRecommendedPlaylist`, the shared `recommendedPlaylistKey`), and Now Playing's "Playing from" returns to it through `pcRouteForContext` (the shared `playbackContextDestination`). **Both pages keep their place:** blocks arrive one provider at a time and the layout can put a new one above those on screen, so while the user hasn't scrolled away from the top the page is scrolled back to its first block whenever the blocks' keys (the shared `RecommendationPageBlock.key`) change (Android: `requestScrollToItem(0)`, with the user's own scrolling, `isScrollInProgress`, deciding whether it's pinned; iOS: `scrollTo` while the content's offset is at the top); a user who has scrolled down is never moved.
- **Concert services**: ticketmaster, seatgeek, bandsintown, songkick — API key config in Settings
- **Filtered out on mobile**: youtube (consent redirect blocks Chromium renderer), ollama (needs local server) — via `capabilities.mobile: false`; and any plugin needing a host bridge the app doesn't supply (see "The plug-in host contract" below)

**The plug-in host contract: `this.host` and required host bridges (desktop parachord#1355, #1561).** Both apps run desktop's `resolver-loader.js` byte for byte (`assets/js/` and `iosApp/Parachord/Resources/`, a test keeps them identical). The loader gives each plugin a frozen `this.host`, built from the supplier in the app's JS bootstrap (the `plugin-host-supplier` block, identical in `bootstrap.html` and `IosJsRuntime.BOOTSTRAP_JS`, `PluginHostContractTest`): `platform`, `appVersion()` (`PluginManager` sets `window.__parachordAppVersion` before loading), `getConfig(id)` (the config `applyResolverConfigs` applied), `openExternal(url)` (https only, `NativeBridge.openExternal` on both), plus each bridge the plugin's `requires.hostBridges` / `optionalHostBridges` names that the app supplies (`bridges`). A plugin whose required bridges the app lacks is **not loaded at all** (`PluginCompat.missingHostBridges`, `PluginManager(hostBridges = …)`), as on desktop: loaded, its URL patterns would claim links it can't open and every resolve would throw `not-ready`. Android supplies `webSession` (below) and `widevine`; iOS supplies `webSession` only (#779), so `amazonmusic.axe` (requires `webSession`, `widevine` optional) loads on both. `PluginManager` writes its `hostBridges` set into `window.__parachordHostBridges` before loading, and the supplier's `bridges` getter hands each listed name `window.__parachordBridge_<name>`. Adding a bridge means two things together: the name in the platform's `PluginManager(hostBridges = …)` and its `window.__parachordBridge_<name>` object in that platform's bootstrap. The `webSession` object is the `web-session-bridge` block, identical in `bootstrap.html` and `IosJsRuntime.BOOTSTRAP_JS` (`PluginHostContractTest`). Plugins no longer get a per-plugin `storage` object (C3's `createPluginStorage` stays in the bootstraps, unused): desktop's loader has none and no plugin used it.

**Plug-in status, the account row and the plug-in switches (#707, desktop parachord#1356 / #1505).** A plugin with `capabilities.status` reports its own state through `getStatus` (`{ signedIn, playbackLimit, statusLabel, statusMessage }`). The shared `PluginStatusStore` (`shared/.../plugin/`, both apps) reads each one through the loader's `getStatus` (`PluginManager.getStatus`, unique-key poll; the loader races it against its 8 s `STATUS_BUDGET_MS` and answers `STATUS_READ_FAILED`): at app start once the plugins load, when Settings opens, when a plugin's sheet opens, and after Sign in / Sign out. A throw, a timeout or the failed marker is a failed read, never a block. `PlaybackLimits.kt` ports desktop's `playback-limits.js` (`pluginStatusLimit`, `pluginStatusLimits`, `pluginStatusRow`, `planBlockedIds`, `notPlayingIds`, `tileStatus`, `limitTransition`, `limitStorageKey`), with its test vectors. A plugin's sheet shows desktop's account row for a status-capable plugin: "X account", the reply's line (`label · message`; a failed read says it couldn't check), and **Sign in** (when not signed in and the plugin implements `signIn`) or **Sign out** (`signOut`, read from the `.axe`'s implementation keys: `PluginInfo.hasSignIn` / `hasSignOut`). Each runs the plugin's method (`PluginManager.callSessionMethod`; sign-in waits up to 10 min for the person, sign-out 30 s), re-reads the status and shows desktop's words ("Signed out of X", or the plugin's error).

**A play refused as not signed in asks to sign in (#775, desktop parachord#1523).** When a plugin refuses a play with `not-signed-in` (Android's DRM manifest, iOS's `getStreamUrl`), the shared `PluginSignInPrompt` (`shared/.../plugin/`) decides, for a plugin with `capabilities.status` and `signIn` only, never by id. First the plugin's hiccup rule: if it implements `signInPromptDecision` and its status says signed in, it may answer "don't prompt" (a session refresh caught a load), and the play retries quietly after 2 s. Otherwise, while a Parachord screen shows (Android: an activity is resumed; iOS: `IosContainer.phoneAppInFront`, from `RootView`'s scene phase), playback stops on the track for Play (a `FAILED` slot, "Sign in to X in Settings to play this track.") and the person is asked "Sign in to X?" (`SignInPromptCopy`; Android `PluginSignInPromptDialog` at the app's root, iOS `RootView`'s alert over `pending`). Sign in runs the plugin's `signIn` through `PluginStatusStore.signIn`, shows its words, and presses Play on the track once if nothing else was played meanwhile; a second not-signed-in for that track is the refused-source rule. Not now, or a sign-in closed without one, leaves the track stopped (desktop's `holdFailedTrack`) and Play asks again; it also quiets the prompt for 10 minutes for plays the queue starts by itself. One question at a time; a second refusal waits for its answer. With no screen (locked, the car), during a manual source switch, or for a plugin that can't sign in, a not-signed-in stays a refused source: another source plays. The question is asked outside the play lock, so a Next while it's up isn't held. Desktop shows its consent dialog only for Amazon (a host flow); every other plugin's sign-in runs at once.

A music plug-in's sheet has one on switch, **Enable X** (`active_resolvers`; on Android the empty list still means all, and turning one off seeds it), and while it's on up to three nested ones, ported from desktop's `plugin-switch.js` (`PluginSwitch.kt`): **Play from X** (`playback_off_resolvers`), **Use metadata from X** (`metadata_off_resolvers`) and **Use recommendations from X** (`recommendations_off_resolvers`, also on meta-services with `capabilities.recommendations`). Each list holds an id only while its switch is off. A meta-service's existing on/off is its "Enable X". The old second "Enabled" switch on music plug-ins is gone; turning Enable on also drops the id from `disabled_plugins` / `disabled_meta_providers`. A plan-blocked plugin's Play from shows off and locked with the limit's message. **A plug-in that needs a sign-in never shows as enabled until it's signed in (#782, desktop parachord#1615).** "Signed in" is the shared `signedInMap` (`PluginAccounts.kt`, desktop's `pluginAccounts`): the host's own sign-ins (Spotify's and SoundCloud's tokens, Apple Music's Music User Token; Android `SettingsViewModel.pluginAccounts`, iOS `SettingsViewModel.hostSignedIn` / `hostSignedOut`, each left out until its check has answered) with each status plug-in's `signedIn` laid over them. Only `false` counts; no entry (no status, a failed read, Last.fm, ListenBrainz) never locks. Signed out, **Enable X** (`enableSwitchState`) reads off and locked, "Sign in to X first" (`signInFirstHint`), even while the plug-in is in `active_resolvers`, and so do Play from, Use metadata and Use recommendations (a plan limit still wins for Play from). Display only: the plug-in stays active and keeps doing what works signed out, and no saved list changes, so signing in shows everything as it was. A disabled switch can't be toggled on either app. **The sheet's order:** a plug-in that needs a sign-in (`accountFirst`: Spotify, SoundCloud, Apple Music, or `capabilities.status`) shows its account and keys first, then Enable, signed in or not; the rest keep Enable first. **The priority list:** an active plug-in that's signed out (`pluginSignedOut`) reads as off (greyed, no Enabled check, "Sign in" in the warning colour, `SIGN_IN_TILE_LABEL`), sits unnumbered with the not-playing ones, isn't draggable, and still opens its sheet; `pluginTileOrder` numbers the rest 1, 2, … with no gap. On iOS a signed-out Spotify, SoundCloud or Apple Music isn't active (it's dropped from the active list while unusable), so only a status plug-in reaches that row there.

**One "not playing" list:** `notPlayingIds(planBlockedIds(limits), playback_off_resolvers + hostUnplayable)` (`PluginStatusStore.notPlaying` / `notPlayingNow`). `hostUnplayable` is the loaded plugins whose only way to play is a plugin-supplied path the host can't run (shared `HostPlayback.kt`: `pluginPlaybackPaths` is `widevine` with the DRM pair, `html5Stream`, and `plainStream` for a Widevine plugin that also implements `getStreamUrl`, desktop parachord#1613; `hostCannotPlay(info, HostPlayback.ANDROID | IOS)`). Android runs all three, so it's empty there; iOS runs `html5Stream` and `plainStream` (AVPlayer, #670), so it holds only a Widevine plugin with no `getStreamUrl` (Amazon Music before 1.23.0): its tile reads "Search and links only" (`tileStatus(…, hostUnplayableIds)`, "Parachord can’t play from it on this device yet") and its Play from is off and locked (`playbackSwitchState(…, hostCannotPlay)`). Amazon Music 1.23.0 plays its purchases on iOS through its plain stream (`playsThroughStreamUrl`). Everything that decides what plays leaves it out, as an extra excluded set beside the active list: `ResolverScoring` (`getNotPlaying`, so selection and the coordinator's ranking), `ResolverBadges` (`badges(notPlaying)`, `hasPlayableSource(sources, notPlaying)`), `SourceReselection.switchableSources(…, notPlaying)`, Home's `playbackResolvers` (`homePlaybackResolvers(notPlaying)`; `HomeSources.playbackLimits` feeds the tiles' limited state), and the Settings priority list, where a not-playing plug-in is an unnumbered tile with `tileStatus`'s label ("Prime: no playback", or "Search and links only"), keeping its `resolver_order` slot. Resolution still asks it (covers, links). "Use metadata from X" off skips the plugin in `MetadataService` (`getMetadataOff`: artist info, photos, covers, track lists; search still asks it) and in the plugin artist-photo step. A drag in the priority list saves `resolver_order_memory` (`ResolverOrderMemory.rememberOrder`), and a re-enabled plug-in goes back where the user ranked it (`insertRemembered`), not to the end. iOS runs the same store, switches and account row; Amazon Music is the status-capable plugin that loads there (#779), with Sign in opening the web-session sign-in sheet.

**A plan that plays only what the account owns (desktop #1494 / #1598).** An unblocked limit can say `ownedOnly: true` (Amazon on every plan but Music Unlimited plays the tracks bought from Amazon); `pluginStatusLimit` carries it only on an unblocked entry that says exactly `true`. The plugin enforces it itself (its search answers only owned tracks, its stream request refuses the rest as plan-ineligible, which plays another source), so the resolver is **not** in the not-playing list: it keeps its number and badges, and its tile shows `tileStatus`'s label ("Prime: purchases only", else "Purchases only"; Android's numbered tile, iOS's numbered row). **Plan changes:** after each successful status read (never a failed one) `PluginStatusStore` runs the shared `limitTransition(lastStored, limit)` against the resolver's remembered state (`SettingsStore.getPlaybackLimitState`, KvStore key `playback_limit_blocked:<id>`, desktop's: `1` blocked, `owned`, `0` playable). Entering `owned` from anything else **purges**: the platform's `clearResolver(id, sourcesToo = true)` drops every saved source of that resolver, found or missed, since a source found on another plan may be a track the account doesn't own. An upgrade to playable from `1` or `owned`, when the limit says `searchesHeldBack`, calls it with `sourcesToo = false` (only the misses go). Android: `TrackResolverCache.clearResolver` (memory and `track_sources_cache.json`; each track left without a found source of it gets a due retry of just that resolver, asked when its row is next on screen, and leaves the saved file, so a restart resolves it from scratch). iOS: `IosTrackResolverCache.clearResolver` (through `IosContainer.resolverSourcesClearer`, set in `AppBootstrap`; its miss and failure back-offs for that resolver go, so the per-row fill asks again). The shared `clearResolverSources` / `asksResolverAgain` decide what goes. The queue keeps its tracks.

**Web sessions: the `webSession` host bridge (desktop parachord#1356).** A cookie-session web service (Amazon Music) is driven by its plugin through capture and replay; the host supplies the generic machinery, to desktop's contract (`docs/architecture/web-session.md` in the desktop repo, `main-process/web-session.js` and `ipc/web-session-bridge.js`). The plugin sees `this.host.webSession` with desktop's preload shape: `load`, `pageFetch`, `fetch`, `state`, `clear`, `signIn`, each a promise that resolves, never rejects, with desktop's `{success, …}` answers and reasons (`load-failed`, `not-ready`, `invalid`, `superseded`). The engine is shared and never names a service: `WebSessionHost` (one per session), `WebSessionRules` (the URL, `doneWhen` and capture-pattern predicates) and `WebSessionBridge` (payload validation and the caps: timeoutMs 60 s, capture budgetMs 30 s, quietMs 10 s) in `shared/.../websession/`; the service's domains live in host config (`WebSessionConfigs.AMAZON_MUSIC`, desktop's `amazon-web-session.js` values). Desktop's five rules hold: (1) loads serialize; (2) a navigation waits out in-flight pageFetches, and a pageFetch during a navigation answers `busy`; (3) the idle park blanks the page 30 s after the last activity, never while busy; (4) captures seen during our own pageFetch are `echo: true`; (5) the capture window ends 1 s after the last capture (the whole 8 s budget with none), at a `doneWhen` match, and returns its captures on a navigation timeout only with `returnOnTimeout`. URL scoping is the protection: `fetch`/`pageFetch`/`load`/`signIn` URLs and capture patterns must be https on an allowed host (exact hosts, narrower than the cookie domains); `fetch` follows redirects itself and refuses a hop off the list; `pageFetch` runs only in a page on an allowed host. A load queued before `clear()` is `superseded` without running. Android (`app/.../websession/`): `AndroidWebSessionPlatform` is a hidden WebView, never attached, laid out at 1100×800 (a 0×0 single-page app never fires its requests), with desktop's Chrome UA; the capture is a document-start script wrapping `fetch` and `XMLHttpRequest` in frames on the cookie domains, reporting through a web message listener scoped to the same origins (without document-start scripts the bridge answers `not-ready`); the page can't navigate off the cookie domains; `pageFetch` uses the unique-key poll; cookies are the app's one `CookieManager` (Apple Music's page shares it), so `clear()` expires only the cookies under the declared domains, never `removeAllCookies`. Sign-in is `WebSessionSignInActivity`, a full-screen WebView on the same `CookieManager` with the config's title and a close button and no capture script; the host ticks it every second and the plugin's `decide` runs in JS (`__pcWsSignInTick` → `NativeBridge.webSessionDecide`). **Shared between the platforms:** the capture script and its report parser (`WebSessionScripts`; each platform only names its sink), the NativeBridge side (`WebSessionJsCalls`: `webSessionCall` / `webSessionDecide`, the callback-id check, the sign-in tick) and the JS bridge block. **iOS (#779)** runs the same engine: `IosWebSessionPlatform` (iosMain) over Swift's `WebSessionWebView` (`iosApp/Parachord/WebSessionWebView.swift`, a callback-style `IosWebSessionView`): a hidden `WKWebView` on the default data store, 1100×800, in the key window behind everything and untouchable, desktop's Chrome UA; the capture is a document-start `WKUserScript` in every frame (the script itself runs only in https frames on the cookie domains) reporting to a `WKScriptMessageHandler` that drops reports from frames off them; a main-frame navigation off the cookie domains, or a popup, is refused; `pageFetch` is `callAsyncJavaScript` in the page world (it awaits the promise, so no poll); `WKHTTPCookieStore` has no per-URL read, so Swift hands Kotlin the jar and the shared `WebSessionCookies` decides the `Cookie` header, `state()`'s names and which cookies `clear()` deletes (only those under the declared domains: the store is shared with the SoundCloud and Bandcamp pages); `fetch` runs on the Darwin client with the response-type guard and no cookie storage of its own. A request with no body declares no length (`NoLengthBody`): Ktor's default empty body made the Darwin engine send `Content-Length: 0` on a GET, which Amazon's edge answers with a bare 400, so `config.json` failed, the session context had no customer or device id, and the purchase stream's request went out with a `null` body (#784; OkHttp drops the header, so Android never saw it). Sign-in is a full-screen sheet with a visible `WKWebView` on the same store, the config's title and a close button, no capture script. Never log a cookie value, header value or request body.

**Minimum app version (#484, desktop parachord#1197).** A plugin may declare `manifest.minAppVersion` = `{ "desktop": "x.y.z", "android": "x.y.z", "ios": "x.y.z" }`, also copied into its `manifest.json` catalog entry; each client reads only its own key. `PluginSyncService` checks the catalog entry before downloading and the `.axe` before `writeCachedPlugin`, reporting refusals in `SyncResult.skipped` (not `failed`, so the 24 h stamp still happens); `PluginManager` skips a cached copy below the floor so the bundled one stays in use, and never checks the bundled copy. Rules live in `shared/.../plugin/PluginCompat.kt`, a port of desktop's `main-process/plugin-compat.js`: no field, no platform entry or a non-`x.y.z` value means no floor; parts compare numerically; the app's own suffix is dropped; an app that can't report its version isn't refused. Both classes take `appVersion` + `platform` from the platform wiring (`BuildConfig.VERSION_NAME` / `"android"`, `CFBundleShortVersionString` / `"ios"`). Unlike `capabilities.mobile: false` (never on mobile), this is a floor for plugins that do run here. Mobile has no manual install, so desktop's "needs Parachord X or later" refusal has no counterpart.

**Native-first resolver strategy:** For spotify/applemusic/localfiles, the native Kotlin resolver always runs first (faster, no JS bridge overhead). .axe resolvers (soundcloud, bandcamp, future resolvers) fill gaps for sources with no native implementation. Both run in parallel via `ResolverManager.resolve()`.

**Key files:** `plugin/JsRuntime.kt`, `plugin/PluginManager.kt`, `plugin/PluginSyncService.kt`, `bridge/JsBridge.kt`, `bridge/NativeBridge.kt`, `ai/providers/AxeAiProvider.kt`, `playback/scrobbler/AxeScrobbler.kt`

### Plugin settings + the html5Stream engine (Subsonic, #401 / plan PR #664)

A plugin that declares `capabilities.html5Stream` streams through **ExoPlayer playing a URL the plugin builds at play time** — desktop's shared HTML5 `<audio>` element contract, with all the Subsonic logic staying in the plugin. The poster child is `subsonic.axe` (a self-hosted Navidrome/Subsonic server); the design is generic (any plugin declaring the capability routes the same way, and a future `widevine` capability slots in beside it).

**Routing is capability-driven, never resolver-id:** `PlaybackRouter` asks `pluginManager.html5StreamPlugin(resolver)`; `PluginStreamHandler : SourceHandler` builds a `MediaItem` from `pluginManager.getStreamUrl(resolver, trackJson)` — the unique-key poll into the resolver-loader (an async **IIFE inside the poll expression**: pollAsync splices it into `var result = <expression>`, so a statement list there is a syntax error and the call never answers — caught only on-device). It's ordinary ExoPlayer playback: seek by Range, `STATE_ENDED` advance, the #614 foreground keep, the car, resume-at-position (#657) — all engine-agnostic machinery. `PlaybackRouter.route` calls `pluginManager.ensureInitialized()` first, so a cold start (Auto, no screen) never routes on an empty plugin list.

**The credentialed URL lives for ONE play.** A Subsonic token+salt pair replays until the password changes, so the URL from `getStreamUrl` reaches ExoPlayer's `MediaItem` and nothing else — never the track, the resolver cache, the saved queue, sync, or an unredacted log line (`PluginStreamUrl.redact` strips `t`/`s`/`token`/`apikey`/`api_key` values; the handler logs redacted only). Because the results can't carry a URL, a plugin result rides on `ResolvedSource.pluginTrackId` (the `<resolverId>Id` convention, `subsonicId`) plus a non-secret placeholder URL (`subsonic:track:<id>`, `pluginTrackUrl`/`parsePluginTrackUrl`) — safe in caches and sync, and what the engine hands back to the plugin. `PluginStreamUrl.isPlayable` is **https-only** for now; the deferred plain-`http://` home-server allowance (plan PR #664 decision 1) grows in that one function plus the network security config.

**Failures:** no URL / not configured / a wrong password → `PlayFailure.START_FAILED` with `checkPluginSettingsMessage` ("Check Subsonic's settings to play this."); a 401/403 from the stream during playback maps the same way (`PlaybackController.authRejectionCode` walks the ExoPlayer cause chain). The plugin carries `minAppVersion.android "0.9.11"` / `.ios "0.3.0"` floors; iOS 0.3.0 is the first to load it.

**iOS (#670).** The same shared pieces, AVPlayer for ExoPlayer. `IosResolverRuntime.resolveOne` keeps a url-less result under the placeholder (and, for a plugin played through `getStreamUrl`, its `pluginFields`). `PlaybackRouter` routes by capability and method (`IosContainer.playsThroughStreamUrl`, shared `playsThroughStreamUrl(info, HostPlayback.IOS)`), asks `IosContainer.pluginStream` at play time (the resolver-loader poll `PluginManager.requestStreamUrl`, its answer read by the shared `PluginStreamReplies` / `pluginStreamOutcome`) and hands the URL to `IosAVPlayer.load` and nothing else; seek, the end-of-track advance and resume at a position are AVPlayer's as for any stream. No URL / not configured → `START_FAILED` with `checkPluginSettingsMessage`; a 401/403 in the item's error log (`IosAVPlayer.failedHttpStatus`) shows the same message. Settings → the plug-in's sheet draws the field editor (`PluginFieldsSection` over `IosContainer.pluginConfigFields` / `savePluginConfig` / `testPluginConnection`; passwords in the Keychain through the shared `PluginUserConfig`), with "Set up" for a plug-in that isn't. Because iOS gates the coordinator on `active_resolvers`, a save that leaves the plug-in configured adds it and a cleared config removes it (shared `activeResolversAfterConfig`), and `AppBootstrap` seeds it once at launch when it's already configured (`seedConfiguredPluginResolvers`). The secrets are read from the Keychain once and kept in memory until a save (`PluginUserConfig.pluginConfigs`' cache), and `isPluginConfigured` never caches an answer from a plug-in host that hasn't loaded the plug-in yet. `subsonic.axe` is bundled in `iosApp/Parachord/Resources/axe-plugins/` (a folder reference; `BundledPluginCopiesTest` keeps both apps' bundles identical), with its logo and its place after `localfiles` in `ResolverBadgeRow`'s order.

**Plugin settings (`PluginUserConfig`, shared):** a plugin's `settings.configurable` fields (Subsonic: serverUrl/username/password) render in a generic field editor in the plugin's Settings sheet, but only for a plugin the app doesn't set up itself (`PluginUserConfig.usesGenericSettings`). Most bundled plugins declare configurable fields for desktop's settings page, so `HOST_CONFIGURED_PLUGIN_IDS` (Spotify, SoundCloud, Apple Music, local files, Last.fm, ListenBrainz, Libre.fm, Discogs, Wikipedia, the AI and concert services) keep their own sheets, get no generic config, never show "Set up", and skip the `isConfigured` gate. Before this, v0.9.11 replaced all of their sheets with the field editor (no Connect button) and sent each of them `{}` over the config the app supplies (SoundCloud's token, the Last.fm and ListenBrainz usernames). For the generic editor: text fields → `plugin_config_<id>` in KvStore, `password` fields → `SecureTokenStore` (one key per secret, never plain prefs). The values feed the plugin through `resolverConfigProvider`, merged field by field after the per-service configs (`mergeResolverConfigs`: a field the user set wins, an unset one leaves the per-service value; a guard for any plugin with generic settings, since a plain map `+` let a plugin's empty `{}` replace the host's whole config), `PluginManager.isPluginConfigured` (the plugin's own `isConfigured`, cached until a save; no answer, e.g. the runtime not ready or the plugin threw, counts as configured there, and as not connected on Home through `isPluginConfiguredOrNull`) gates resolution — an unset-up plugin answers null without a request, so it records no miss — and a **Test connection** button runs the plugin's `search("test")` with the status in any error (the #1358 throw-on-failure change makes wrong credentials say so). A `requiresAuth` plugin that isn't configured shows "Set up" on its tile. A failed search THROWS rather than answering `[]` on desktop's plugin (Parachord/parachord#1358, `subsonic.axe` 0.3.3), so leaving home Wi-Fi is retried ("couldn't answer"), never recorded as misses (#566).

**Key files:** `playback/handlers/PluginStreamHandler.kt`, `shared/.../plugin/PluginUserConfig.kt`, `shared/.../playback/PluginStreamUrl.kt`, `shared/.../plugin/PluginManager.kt` (`getStreamUrl`, `isPluginConfigured`, `searchForConnectionTest`, `html5StreamPlugin`), `shared/.../resolver/ResolverModels.kt` (`pluginTrackId`, `pluginTrackUrl`), `app/.../ui/screens/settings/SettingsScreen.kt` (`PluginFieldsConfig`), `app/.../playback/PlaybackController.kt` (the `PluginStreamStartException` catch + 401/403 mapping), desktop `plugins-src/subsonic/` (the plugin itself — v0.3.3)

### Plugin-supplied Widevine playback (Android; Amazon Music, desktop #1356 M4)

**On iOS the same pair plays as FairPlay HLS (#794, desktop #1627, plugin 1.24.0).** iOS has no Widevine, so its plug-in's `getPlaybackManifest` (asked by a host whose `this.host.platform` is `ios`) answers `{ type: 'hls-fairplay', url, certificate, headers: { manifest, segments, key }, context }`. The bootstrap's `__pcDrm` (one `// <plugin-drm>` block, identical in `bootstrap.html` and `IosJsRuntime.BOOTSTRAP_JS`, `PluginHostContractTest`) takes an `accept` argument (`DRM_ACCEPT_WIDEVINE` on Android, `DRM_ACCEPT_FAIRPLAY` on iOS) and hands back the URL, the certificate and the playlist / segment headers; the plugin's context and key headers stay in JS. The shared `HostPlayback.FAIRPLAY` path (`playsThroughFairPlay`) makes the pair iOS's first choice, the plain stream its fallback (`playsThroughStreamUrl` no longer picks a DRM plug-in on iOS), and `PluginDrmReplies.openFairPlay` / `PluginFairPlayStream` read the reply. The engine is iOS-only: see `iosApp/AGENTS.md` → "A plug-in's FairPlay HLS". A plugin's `getStreamUrl` gets up to 30 s on both apps (`STREAM_URL_TIMEOUT_MS`; desktop sets no limit): Amazon's reads the purchases first, about 8 s on a fresh session.

A plugin declaring `capabilities.widevine` that implements BOTH `getPlaybackManifest(track, config)` and `relayLicense(challengeB64, context)` (desktop's "plugin-supplied Widevine path", docs/architecture/plugins.md; checked by presence, `PluginInfo.hasDrmPair` / `PluginManager.widevinePlugin`, never by id) plays through ExoPlayer with Widevine DRM. `amazonmusic.axe` is the first. iOS has no Widevine; there the same pair plays as FairPlay HLS, with the plugin's `getStreamUrl` as its fallback (see "On iOS the same pair plays as FairPlay HLS" and "A plain stream beside the pair").

**A plain stream beside the pair (desktop parachord#1613).** A Widevine plugin may also implement `getStreamUrl(track, config)` (`PluginInfo.hasStreamUrl`, by presence; Amazon Music 1.23.0 answers it for the account's purchases, a 256 kbps MP3, and declares no `html5Stream`). Android tries Widevine first; when the DRM stream doesn't start it plays the plain stream through ExoPlayer on the same source, at the same position (`PlaybackController.playPlainStreamInstead`, `PluginStreamHandler.plainStreamItem`, routed with `PlaybackRouter.plainStreamAction`). The shared `drmPlainStreamFallbackAllowed(outcome, started, hasStreamUrl)` decides: a refused licence, a manifest past its budget after the one retry, a failed load, a family refusal past its retry and a start wait that timed out fall back; a plan refusal, not-signed-in, a newer play and a play that had started never do. A `null` or a throw from `getStreamUrl` leaves the DRM failure's own handling and message, and a manual switch that fails both ways still gives the old playback back. On iOS the same plugin plays its plain stream on its own (`HostPlayback.PLAIN_STREAM`): a `null` (not a purchase) is a refused source (`noPlainStreamMessage`), `not-signed-in` and a plan refusal map as the DRM path's (`PluginDrmMessages.refused`), and a failed request is `START_FAILED`.

- **JS (`bootstrap.html`, Android only): `window.__pcDrm`** `open(resolverId, track, budgetMs)` calls the plugin's `getPlaybackManifest` with the whole track (its `sources` map: the plugin's own record is its result's fields, `ResolvedSource.pluginFields`, kept only for widevine plugins, `pluginResultFields`) within desktop's 10 s budget (`PluginDrmContract.MANIFEST_BUDGET_MS`) and keeps the plugin's `context` in a JS map keyed by a new session id, so the context never reaches Kotlin; `license(sessionId, challengeB64)` calls `relayLicense(challenge, context)` (15 s); `event` the plugin's `handlePlayerEvent`; `close`. Each answers `{ok, …}` or `{ok:false, error:{reason, planKey, licenseDenied, denialReason, message}}`; Kotlin reaches them through `PluginManager.openDrmSession` / `relayDrmLicense` / `sendDrmPlayerEvent` / `closeDrmSession` (the unique-key poll). `this.host.widevine` (the plugin's optional bridge) is `{ stop }`, which stops nothing: the host owns the engine, and a cleanup runs on every hot-reload.
- **Engine (`app/.../playback/drm/`).** `PluginDrmHandler` (routed first in `PlaybackRouter`, ahead of the `stream: false` check) opens the session and answers a `parachord-drm://<sessionId>` MediaItem; `PluginDrmSessions` (Koin single) holds sessionId → manifest, resolver and the quality settings read at the play's start. The service's ExoPlayer has `PluginDrmMediaSourceFactory` (that scheme → `DashMediaSource` over the in-memory manifest, segment URLs as given, `DefaultDrmSessionManager` with `C.WIDEVINE_UUID`, licences through `PluginDrmLicenseCallback`, provisioning by the standard Widevine POST; every other item → `DefaultMediaSourceFactory`, unchanged) and `PluginDrmTrackSelector`. Otherwise it's an ordinary ExoPlayer track: seek, `STATE_ENDED` advance, the #614 foreground keep, resume at a position (`setMediaItems(…, startMs)`).
- **Quality (shared `PlaybackQuality.kt`, desktop's `widevine-quality.js` policy and test vectors).** Tiers are bandwidth ceilings (saver ≤ 100k, standard ≤ 200k, high ≤ 500k, max = best), `normalize` maps earlier names; families flac/opus/aac, `ec-3` only with "Prefer Dolby Atmos mixes", AC-4 and MPEG-H never. Keys `playback_quality` (default `max`), `playback_abr` (true), `playback_spatial` (false) on `SettingsStore`; Settings → General → Playback Quality (Android only: nothing on iOS reads them; iOS's plug-in sheets show who sets each service's quality instead). The selector applies it only while the player is on a session: locked = the tier's rung; adaptive = the tracks of the picked rung's adaptation set at or below the cap. ExoPlayer adapts within one group, and the manifests put each family in its own set, so adaptive roams one family's ladder (desktop roams across families). A codec the decoder doesn't handle is never chosen (ExoPlayer's capabilities stand in for desktop's probe). **Atmos only where something can play it, by either of Android's two routes (Dolby's guide for car and phone media apps):** a decoder in the phone (`AudioDecoders` reads `MediaCodecList` once for `audio/eac3` and `audio/eac3-joc`), or pass-through, where the current output takes the undecoded stream and decodes it itself (a car's amplifier on Android Automotive, an HDMI soundbar; `AudioTrack.isDirectPlaybackSupported` for `ENCODING_E_AC3` / `ENCODING_E_AC3_JOC`, Android 10 and later, asked on every read since it changes with the output). ExoPlayer picks pass-through when both exist. Shared `DeviceAudioCapabilities` holds all four and `canPlayAtmos` is any of them; with none, General shows "This phone can’t play Dolby Atmos" in place of the switch and the play's settings drop the preference (`PlaybackQualitySettings.forDevice`, read at each play's start), so spatial-on can't strand a play (a Pixel 9a has no decoder). Android Auto's announced Atmos (May 2026) has no developer docs yet; if it arrives as a pass-through output, this check covers it.
- **Who follows it, in words (shared `PlaybackQualityLine.kt`).** Each music plug-in's sheet shows a "Playback quality" line under its nested switches while Enable is on (`PlaybackQualityLine.forPlugin(plugin, platform, settings, device)`). `Follows(summary)` is decided by capability: a Widevine plug-in with both DRM methods (Android only), and Apple Music on Android, whose MusicKit JS takes the tier (Data saver → MusicKit's standard 64 kbps, every other tier its high 256 kbps: shared `MusicKitQuality`, desktop's `musicKitBitrateForQuality`; `MusicKitWebBridge.applyBitrate` → the page's `setBitrate`, after every configure and when the setting changes). The summary is desktop's tier label ("Max: best available, lossless where playable"), plus " · Adjust to your connection" (Widevine only, abr on) and " · Dolby Atmos when available" (spatial on and the phone decodes it); tapping it opens Settings › General. `ExternalControl(title, detail)` otherwise: Spotify "Set in the Spotify app", SoundCloud and Bandcamp "Set by its player", an `html5Stream` plug-in (Subsonic) "Set by your server", local files "Plays the file as it is", Apple Music on iOS "Set in iOS Settings"; any other plug-in shows nothing. General's description and the Adjust / Atmos hints come from the same rule over the loaded plug-ins (`PlaybackQualityCopy.build`: "Apple Music uses these settings. Other services choose their own quality.", "<Widevine plug-ins> only.").
- **Now Playing's quality chip.** `PluginDrmTelemetry` reads the service's ExoPlayer's selected audio format while it's on a session (`codecFamilyOf(codecs, sampleMimeType)`) and the shared `qualityLabel(family, isSpatial)` names it ("Dolby Atmos", "Lossless", else nothing) into `PlaybackState.playingQualityLabel`; Now Playing draws it as a small badge under the source picker. iOS has no DRM engine, so no chip yet; the rule is shared for when it does. **The car hears it too:** while the label is Dolby Atmos (shared `isImmersiveQuality`), the media session's playing item carries the Car App Library's immersive-audio extra (`KEY_IMMERSIVE_AUDIO`, `androidx.car.app.mediaextensions.KEY_IMMERSIVE_AUDIO` = 1, spelled in `MediaItemMapping.kt` since we don't ship that library), so a car that reads it leaves out sound effects that would interfere ("Indicate audio format", Android for Cars). The forwarding player's queue snapshot sets it on the playing item only and announces its change itself, since Media3's `MediaMetadata.equals` leaves extras out. Dolby's logo icons (`KEY_CONTENT_FORMAT_TINTABLE_*_ICON_URI`) aren't set: they need Dolby's logo files and terms.
- **Start and failures (shared `pluginDrmOutcome`).** A session has started only once a licence for it came back AND the player plays (`PluginDrmStartRules.started`, for the controller's `awaitDrmStart`, 30 s, desktop's `untilAudible`, and for `play-started`): ExoPlayer plays a stream's clear lead before it needs a key, so on a Pixel a FLAC rung whose licence was refused played 30 s and only then raised `ERROR_CODE_DRM_LICENSE_ACQUISITION_FAILED`. Each licence answer is announced as it lands (`PluginDrmSessions.licenceEvents`): a refusal during the start wait ends it at once, and a per-content refusal for the session on the player after the start latches the family and replays the track on the same source at the same position, once. A DRM player error for a session is classified from that session's recorded refusal whether or not the start wait has ended (`onDrmPlayerError`, `playerErrorOutcome`); with none recorded it's the ordinary player failure. `plan-ineligible` and `not-signed-in` re-read the plugin's status (`PluginStatusStore.refresh`, so a plan limit lands in "not playing") and, like a build's licence refusal (`licenseDenied`), are a refused source: another source plays (the plan's toast once per plugin per session, in the plugin's words), else the refused-embed rule (skip while advancing, else stop with the message). A per-content refusal (`denialReason` `CUSTOMER_NOT_ELIGIBLE`) latches the codec family whose key was asked for (`FamilyRecordingDrmSessionManager`, shared `DrmFamilyTable`) and retries the track once without it; a repeat fails. The refused families are an account fact (desktop's `clearPlanBlocks`, #777): `PluginStatusStore` clears them (its `clearDrmPlanBlocks`, Android `PluginDrmSessions.clearPlanBlocks`, a no-op on iOS) on Sign out (before the plugin's `signOut`), after a successful Sign in, and after a successful status read whose sign-in or plan differs from the last successful one (else from the remembered plan state); never on a failed read. A budget, `load-failed` or `playback-failed` is `START_FAILED`. **A manifest whose 10 s budget ran out is asked for once more, at once (desktop parachord#1603):** the plugin's `getPlaybackManifest` keeps running in JS past the budget (Amazon serializes a purchases read and a session-header mint), so the second call joins it (8.7 s on a Pixel). The bootstrap's `withBudget` marks its own timeout (`budgetExpired`, read into `PluginDrmError.budgetExpired`), and `PluginDrmHandler.createMediaItem` retries only when the shared `manifestRetryAllowed(error, retriesSoFar)` allows it: the host's budget, not a failure the plugin reported (plan-ineligible, not-signed-in, load-failed, a licence refusal), once per play (`MANIFEST_BUDGET_RETRIES`), never for a superseded play (the play's supersession cuts the route, retry included). The budget stays desktop's 10 s; the retry is logged under `PluginDrm`. A newer play supersedes a plugin DRM start at once (desktop's play token, `PlaySupersession`): every play takes a generation the moment it's asked for, before it waits for the play ahead of it, and the start's waits (the manifest, the start wait, the family replay) are cut the moment a newer one exists. The cut play never reaches the player (its item is stopped, its session closed) and isn't a failure, so a Next or a second source switch doesn't queue behind it. `not-signed-in` asks to sign in first when it can (#775, see "A play refused as not signed in asks to sign in"). Not ported: desktop's decoder-append family latch.
- **Telemetry (`PluginDrmTelemetry` on the service's ExoPlayer, shared `PluginPlayerEventTracker`).** Desktop's events and payloads to the plugin's `handlePlayerEvent`, for the session the player is on: `play-started` (asin, albumAsin, durationMs, initialDelaySeconds, variantFacts, cdnType), `seek`, `pause`, `resume`, `ended`, `stopped` when the player leaves a session that played.
- **Debug aid:** `adb logcat -s PluginDrm` shows session opens (resolver, manifest length, variant counts by family), licence round trips (status only), the selected variant and refusals (reason, planKey, denialReason). Never the manifest, a challenge, a licence, a signed URL or a cookie.

**Key files:** `shared/.../playback/drm/{PlaybackQuality,PluginDrm}.kt`, `app/.../playback/drm/*`, `app/src/main/assets/js/bootstrap.html` (`__pcDrm`), `shared/.../plugin/PluginManager.kt` (`widevinePlugin`, the four DRM calls).

### Extensibility principle — abstract over the plugin registry, don't hardwire services

**Default to the plugin/resolver capabilities as the abstraction; never hardwire a
specific service's hosts or logic into a feature.** When a feature needs "given a
provider URL/entity, do X" (resolve a track, fetch a playlist, list models, scrobble),
route it through the resolver-loader's capability surface — `findResolverForUrl(url)`,
`getUrlType(url)`, `resolve`, `lookupUrl`/`lookupAlbum`/`lookupPlaylist`, `listModels`
— so **whichever `.axe` declares matching `urlPatterns` + implements that capability
handles it. Adding a new streaming service should be a plugin drop-in (one `.axe` with
`urlPatterns` + the capability), needing ZERO feature code.** A hardcoded
`when (host) { "open.spotify.com" -> … ; "music.apple.com" -> … }` is the anti-pattern:
it forks per service, goes stale, and blocks new providers. (This is the same spirit as
Common Mistakes #6 — don't reimplement plugin logic natively — applied to *dispatch*.)

- **Native fast-paths are an optimization, not the gate.** It's fine to keep a native
  Spotify/Apple path for speed, but the *fallback* must be registry-driven so unlisted
  providers still work. Example: `ProviderPlaylistResolver` (playlist URL → tracks) does
  native Spotify/Apple first, then `lookupPlaylist` via the registry — so SoundCloud
  (#281) and any future resolver work with no per-service branch.
- **Importing a playlist from a link goes through the same resolver.** The shared
  `PlaylistUrlImporter` (`shared/.../playlist/PlaylistUrlImporter.kt`) is what both apps'
  import field and `parachord://import?url=` call (Android `PlaylistImportManager.importFromUrl`,
  iOS `IosContainer.importPlaylistFromUrl`). An Achordion playlist imports as its XSPF
  endpoint (hosted, polled); a link `ProviderPlaylistResolver` resolves is saved as a
  snapshot with `source = "<providerId>-import"` (`spotify-import`, `applemusic-import`,
  `soundcloud-import`: the provider comes from `ResolvedProtocolPlay.providerId`) and the
  native ids the tracklist carries (`ProtocolTrack.spotifyId` / `appleMusicId`, plus album
  id, length and cover); anything else is a hosted XSPF (`HostedXspfService.importHostedXspf`). A link a plugin claims (`PluginManager.playlistLookupPluginName`) whose lookup fails is an error (`PlaylistLookupFailedException`, "Couldn’t load that playlist from X. Try again."), never an XSPF fetch, as on desktop: Amazon answers a plain fetch of its page with 400 (#778). A plugin's `lookupPlaylist` gets up to 60 s on both apps (`PLAYLIST_LOOKUP_TIMEOUT_MS`; desktop sets no limit): Amazon's waits behind its other page loads, and took 9.6 s in the iOS simulator.
  **The same link is the same playlist (#778, desktop's `hosted-<hash(url)>`).** Its id is
  `<source>-link-<fingerprint of the link>` (`linkImportKey`: the link without its fragment or
  `si` / `utm_*` parameters), and the row's `sourceContentHash` holds a fingerprint of the link's
  tracklist (`linkTracklistSignature`; a link import has no `sourceUrl`, so the XSPF poller never
  reads it). Importing the link again returns that playlist: unchanged since the last import, it
  stays as it is, with the user's edits; changed, its tracks are replaced by the link's
  (`LibraryRepository.replaceLinkImportTracks`, flagged for sync like any edit). Before this every
  import of a link made a new playlist. Imports from before keep their `<source>-<uuid>` ids.
  The id is one no provider owns: sync reads a
  playlist's provider from its id only through the shared `impliedPlaylistProvider`
  (`shared/.../sync/PlaylistIdProvider.kt`), which answers null for `<provider>-import-…`
  ids (saved recommendations' `-import-rec-…` too). So a link import has no pull source,
  no mirror chip from its id and no mode=ALL auto-mirror (`autoMirrorsByDefault` false);
  it can be mirrored by hand to any service but the one it came from.
  Both apps used to choose by URL shape (`open.spotify.com` / `music.apple.com`, else XSPF),
  so a SoundCloud set was fetched as XSPF and failed. A Spotify link imports without a
  Spotify connection too, from the public embed page (tracks keep their Spotify ids).
- **Reaching the `.axe` from an arbitrary URL needs the unique-key POLL on BOTH runtimes.**
  A bare `(async () => …)()` does NOT return the resolved value once it awaits a real
  `fetch` — iOS JSC yields `[object Promise]`, and Android WebView yields the unresolved
  Promise serialized as `{}` (the `Plugin resolve(bandcamp) raw result: {}` artifact).
  Use the proven pattern: a setup `evaluate` writes `window.__resolveResults['<key>'] =
  JSON.stringify(result)` from a fire-and-forget async IIFE, then poll
  `evaluate("window.__resolveResults['<key>']")`. iOS returns the JS string raw; Android
  double-encodes → `.unquote()`. See `IosResolverRuntime.resolveOne`/`lookupPlaylist`
  and `PluginManager.lookupPlaylist`.
- **Audit the plugin layer before assuming a capability exists.** A resolver "just
  works" only if its `.axe` implements the capability — e.g. only spotify/apple/youtube
  `.axe` had `lookupPlaylist`; SoundCloud needed it added (v1.1.0, #281). Many resolvers
  also need credentials (SoundCloud OAuth `config.token`) and return null without them.

- **The host supplies the hooks plugins expect.** `applemusic.axe` asks the host for
  `appleMusicSearchWithMusicKit` (search → resolve), `appleMusicLookupSong`,
  `appleMusicLookupAlbum` (URL lookups + the #424 album fallback) and
  `appleMusicLookupPlaylist` before its own iTunes Search / Lookup. Desktop answers them
  through MusicKit; Android's `bootstrap.html` and iOS's `IosJsRuntime.BOOTSTRAP_JS` port
  them onto `NativeBridge.appleMusicCatalogGet` → shared `AppleMusicCatalog`
  (developer-token catalog GET, `/v1/catalog/` paths only, the app-wide
  `MusicKitCatalogLimiter`), falling back to iTunes like desktop. On iOS the callable is
  attached from Swift (`JsPolyfills.installNativeBridge`) and forwards to
  `IosContainer.appleMusicCatalogGet`; the limiter is shared with `IosResolverRuntime`'s
  native catalog resolve. A fifth hook, `appleMusicSearchAlbumsWithMusicKit(term,
  storefront, limit)`, backs the plugin's `searchAlbums` (the album page's catalog
  fallback search, `applemusic.axe` ≥ 1.2.0). Unlike the other four, it does NOT fall
  back to iTunes itself: it returns the catalog's albums (an empty array is a real miss),
  or `null` when the catalog can't be reached, and the plugin then asks iTunes. iTunes
  album search missed "Mount Zero" (Swapmeet) for over three months after release. iTunes Search lags new releases by days or weeks, so
  without the hooks a just-released song never resolved to Apple Music. Android's native
  Apple Music resolver uses the plugin's `resolve` as its fallback when the catalog can't
  be reached (see "Apple Music resolution — the shared catalog search").
- **Plugin results don't all carry `url`.** ID-based resolvers return a catalog id
  (`appleMusicId`, `spotifyUri`); Bandcamp returns `bandcampUrl` and no `url`. Hosts read
  `url`, then the plugin's own `<resolverId>Url` (`pluginServiceUrl`), then the
  service URL/id fields — never require `url`.
- **Artist photos are a chain with a plugin step** (desktop parachord#1141).
  `MetadataService.getArtistImage` asks Apple Music, Spotify (each only while its plug-in is on, #701), then the active resolvers
  declaring `capabilities.artistImage` (resolver-loader `getArtistImageResolvers` /
  `searchArtistImage`, via `PluginManager`), then Deezer's public search, Wikipedia,
  Discogs, Last.fm and MusicBrainz. First usable photo wins (`ArtistImageChain.isUsableUrl`
  refuses credential-bearing URLs and Last.fm's placeholder); a miss is remembered 6 h only
  when every step answered, so image steps must THROW on failure, not return null.

**Key files:** `shared/.../deeplink/ProviderPlaylistResolver.kt` (registry fallback),
`shared/.../plugin/PluginManager.kt#lookupPlaylist` (Android poll), `shared/iosMain/.../IosResolverRuntime.kt#lookupPlaylist` (iOS poll), `parachord-plugins/<service>.axe` (the per-service capability source of truth).

### On-the-fly Track Resolution

Tracks from external sources (ListenBrainz weekly playlists, AI recommendations, DJ chat) arrive as metadata-only `TrackEntity` objects — they have title/artist/album but no `resolver`, `sourceUrl`, `spotifyUri`, or streaming IDs. These tracks must be resolved through the resolver pipeline before playback.

**Two-layer resolution strategy:**
1. **Background pre-resolution:** Call `TrackResolverCache.resolveInBackground(tracks)` when tracks are loaded into a list. This populates the cache with resolver results so (a) resolver badges appear in the UI and (b) playback starts instantly when the user taps a track.
2. **On-the-fly fallback:** `PlaybackController.playTrackInternal` detects tracks with no source info and calls `resolveOnTheFly()` before routing. This catches any tracks that weren't pre-resolved (e.g. user tapped before background resolution finished).

Results are cached in `TrackResolverCache.putSources()` so subsequent plays of the same track (or queue advancement) reuse the cached sources without re-resolving.

### Resolver Pipeline Rules

**Every tracklist must go through the resolver pipeline.** Any screen that displays a list of playable tracks must resolve each row as it comes on screen (see "Resolution is visibility-scoped" below). This ensures:
- Resolver badges (Spotify, SoundCloud, local files, etc.) appear in the UI
- Playback starts instantly when the user taps a track
- The user's resolver priority preferences are respected

**Resolved sources persist across restarts (#452).** `TrackResolverCache` keeps every resolver's sources (Spotify, Apple Music, SoundCloud, Bandcamp, local files, any plugin) in `track_sources_cache.json` via `PersistentJsonCache` — 7-day TTL and 8,000 entries, matching desktop's track-sources cache. Saved sources load at startup (`awaitLoaded()`, which `resolveInBackground` and `PlaybackController` wait on), so a cold start (Android Auto) routes by what was resolved before and already-resolved tracks aren't searched again. Empty answers stay in memory only. Before this the cache was memory-only and every cold start began empty.

**A resolver that couldn't answer is not a miss (#566).** Every resolver returns null when it answered with no match (or isn't connected) and throws when it couldn't answer: offline, a rate-limit cooldown, a failed or unreadable response, a plugin that threw or timed out (`ResolverRuntime`, both Spotify lambdas, `PluginManager.resolve`). The coordinator catches each one separately and reports it (`ResolveReport.failed`, from `resolveScoredReport` / `resolveRankedWithHintsReport`); the list-returning methods are unchanged. Before this every error was a null, so a resolve with the network down kept only the stored Apple Music id a track carried, and Recent Loves showed Apple Music alone for the 7 days the cache keeps sources. Desktop already did this: an errored resolver gets no entry and is asked again. Android's `TrackResolverCache` keeps a resolve with failures in memory but not on disk, and asks only the failed resolvers again once the back-off passes (2, 4, 8 … 60 minutes, `ResolverRetry`, shared), merging what they find (`ResolverRetry.merge`: a stored-id source stays and takes the retry's artwork). It runs those retries itself (`sweepRetries`, on the background lane) rather than waiting for a screen to submit the track again, since Home only re-submits when its list changes. A play in the background counts: with the screen off and nothing playing, Android's background block keeps the app offline, so retries keep failing (and nothing is saved) until the app is in front or playing. Once, at startup, saved entries made only of the ids a track carried (`ResolverRetry.isStoredIdOnly`) are dropped so the tracks the old code saved that way resolve again (`track_sources_stored_id_purge_566`). Screens that resolve their own rows use `TrackResolverCache.resolveTrack`, not `resolveWithHints` + `putSources`, so their failures are tracked too. iOS's per-row fill already re-asks missing resolvers; it now waits the failure back-off after a throw and 30 minutes after a no-match (`IosTrackResolverCache`).

**Resolution is visibility-scoped, on both apps (#177, #181).** Desktop's `ResolutionScheduler` (`src/renderer/app.js`) resolves a page's rows as they come into view (viewport + overscan) and aborts the ones that scroll away; it never resolves a whole list on open. Bulk-submitting a list fans out a resolver cascade per track (a Spotify and an Apple Music catalog search each); on a fresh client with no cached IDs that tripped Spotify's account-wide abuse window (`429 Retry-After: 2656s` on the shared `client_id`).
- **Android:** each row calls `ResolveWhileVisible(key) { vm.resolveVisible(track) }` (`ui/components/ResolveWhileVisible.kt`): a `DisposableEffect` that starts the row's resolve when the lazy list composes it and cancels the job when the row leaves, or the screen does. The ViewModel's `resolveVisible` returns that job: `TrackResolverCache.resolveInBackground(listOf(track))` (or `resolvePlaylistTracksInBackground`) for screens whose badges come from the cache (Home recent loves, Library songs, Search's Library rows, the queue, playlist detail, weekly playlists, Recommendations), `resolveTrackForRow` for screens that keep their own source map (History, a friend's page, artist top tracks, album, Pop of the Tops). Both run on the cache's priority lane, at most 4 at a time, and skip tracks already resolved, so scrolling back is free. Add a row to a new tracklist the same way; never submit the whole list.
- **iOS:** each row's `.onAppear` calls `IosTrackResolverCache.resolve(_:order:)` (lowest `order` first, a shared worker cap) and its `.onDisappear` calls `leave(artist:title:)`, which drops the row's queued work; a resolve already running finishes.
- **Not visibility-scoped on purpose:** `PlaybackController` resolves the current track, the next few queued tracks and the spinoff pool's next tracks (playback lookahead), and links resolve what they play.
- **The idle background sweep** (desktop's `background` context): the shared `BackgroundResolveSweep` (`shared/.../resolver/BackgroundResolveSweep.kt`) picks the collection's tracks, then every playlist's, that have no source: none cached and no stored Spotify / Apple Music / SoundCloud id or local file on the row (synced tracks count as resolved, as on desktop). Nothing for 30 s after launch or within 3 s of a touch; batches of 5 for the first three, then 25; each track once per run; with nothing to do it looks again after 60 s. The platform asks for the next batch only once its resolver cache is idle and runs it behind every visible row: Android `BackgroundResolveSweeper` (started and stopped with `MainActivity`'s resume and pause, touches from `dispatchTouchEvent`, the cache's background lane), iOS `BackgroundResolveSweeper` (in `IosTrackResolverCache.swift`, while the scene is active, touches from a gesture recognizer on the window that never takes them). Results are saved like any resolve, so the next look reads them from the cache.
- **A local track that plays finds its other sources (#769, desktop parachord#1604).** The sweep skips local files on purpose, and a play routes the file without resolving, so a track whose only playable source is a local file never got any other, and Now Playing's source switcher offered nothing else. When such a track starts (shared `LocalPlayFill.shouldFill`: it plays from `localfiles` and its cache entry has no playable source from another resolver), each app asks the resolvers once per track per session (`PlayFillTracker`), after the play began, and adds what they found to its resolver cache (`mergeFoundSources`: never replaces a source the track has; misses and below-floor finds are dropped). Android: `PlaybackController.fillLocalTrackSources`, off the main thread, into `TrackResolverCache`; iOS: `IosTrackResolverCache.fillLocalPlay`, from `playTrack`'s `.played` branch.

**Every implemented resolver must be in the pipeline.** `ResolverManager.resolve()` must include all resolvers that have working implementations. The `_resolvers` list must match — only list resolvers with actual `resolve*()` methods. Native: `spotify`, `applemusic`, `localfiles`; SoundCloud and Bandcamp resolve through their `.axe`, and `subsonic` through its `html5Stream` capability, gated on its config being complete; the coordinator fans out to every resolve-capable plugin. `youtube` exists in `CANONICAL_RESOLVER_ORDER` and `DEFAULT_RESOLVER_ORDER` but is filtered on mobile (`capabilities.mobile: false`).

**Only call active resolvers.** `ResolverManager.resolve()` checks `settingsStore.getActiveResolvers()` before calling each resolver. If the active list is empty, all implemented resolvers run. If the user has configured a specific set, only those are called. `ResolverScoring.selectBest()` also filters by active resolvers, and leaves out the "not playing" list (a plan limit or "Play from X" off, #707), which resolution still asks.

**Always pass `targetTitle` and `targetArtist` to `resolve()`.** The local file resolver requires both fields for exact matching. All call sites should pass these parameters — without them, local file resolution silently skips. For `resolveWithHints()` these parameters are already required by convention.

**The Library section of Search is matched in Kotlin, not SQL (#529, desktop parachord#1274).** `LibraryTrackSearch` (shared, used by `TrackDao.search` and `searchOnce`) matches a phrase inside the title, artist or album, or every word of the query as a whole word somewhere in those fields, so `Portishead Roads` finds the track and "go it" doesn't find Gorillaz "Sit Down". Ranked as desktop: exact "artist title", then phrase in title, artist, album, then all-words rows. SQLite's `LIKE` only folds ASCII case, so SQL couldn't match "КИНО" to "Кино" without normalized columns and a migration on both platforms. Every row is folded on each search, so plain-ASCII tags skip the regex and the normalizer: at 100,000 tracks a search took about 0.5 s on the iOS simulator with an optimized binary (18 s in a debug test binary before the fast path) and 50 ms on the JVM. Album search still uses its own SQL.

**Search session — shared (#590).** The Search screen's rules live once in `SearchSession` (`shared/.../search/SearchSession.kt`), which both apps drive: Android's `SearchViewModel` hands it the field's text and maps its `SearchState` to the StateFlows `SearchScreen` reads; iOS's `SearchViewModel` gets one from `IosContainer.makeSearchSession()` and watches it with `watchSearch` (flattened to `IosSearchState`). A query searches once it has 2 characters after trimming (`MIN_QUERY_LENGTH`, desktop `handleSearchInput`); below that the results clear and recent searches show. It searches 300 ms (`DEBOUNCE_MS`) after the last keystroke, and a keystroke cancels the running search at once, so its MusicBrainz requests leave the 1 req/s queue (#475). It shows library tracks and library albums (Flows, so they follow library changes; both apps list them as "Library" and "Library albums", five each), the three remote categories as their providers answer (`MetadataService.search*Progressively` through `SearchCatalog`, ranked by `SearchRanking`, artists from `SearchArtistList`), and covers for rows that came without a usable one (`SearchArtwork`; keys from `trackArtKey` / `albumArtKey` / `artistArtKey`): the first 10 of each remote category as they arrive, and any other row once it's on screen (`trackShown` / `albumShown` / `artistShown`, called from each row: Android a `LaunchedEffect`, iOS `.onAppear`), once per row per query. A track row's last resort is the cover of the source it resolved to, which Android passes in (`resolvedTrackArt`); iOS's rows draw it themselves (`pcTrackArt`). Resolving the remote rows stays per platform: Android resolves them once their search finishes (`onRemoteTracksSettled` → `TrackResolverCache.resolveTrack`), iOS resolves the rows on screen (`IosTrackResolverCache`, `.onAppear`). Tests: `SearchSessionTest` (commonTest, JVM + iOS).

### Resolver Badge Display — Confidence-Aware Icons

Resolver badges in the UI must reflect match confidence, matching the desktop's approach:

**One shared rule (#592): `ResolverBadges` (`shared/.../resolver/ResolverBadges.kt`).** Android's `TrackResolverCache.trackResolvers` / `trackResolverConfidences` and iOS's `ResolverBadgeRow` both use `ResolverBadges.badges(sources, order, active, disabled, notPlaying)`, as desktop's list views draw them: playable sources only (not `noMatch`, at or above the 0.60 floor), from resolvers the user has on (in the active list when there is one; not disabled; not in the "not playing" list, a plan limit or "Play from X" off: desktop badges only `playbackResolvers`), one badge per resolver (its best source), in the user's order then confidence. The dim check is `ResolverBadges.isDimmed` (at or below `DIM_AT_OR_BELOW`, 0.80); desktop dims below 0.95 since May 2026. iOS greys out a row with `hasPlayableSource(sources, notPlaying)`.

1. **Filter noMatch from display.** Sources with confidence < `MIN_CONFIDENCE_THRESHOLD` (0.60) are excluded from the resolver icon list entirely. A wrong-song Spotify result (0.50 confidence) must not show a Spotify badge — otherwise the user sees a Spotify icon but tapping plays a local file (or nothing), which is confusing.

2. **Dim low-confidence icons.** Sources with confidence ≤ 0.80 render at 60% opacity (`alpha 0.6`). Full-confidence matches (> 0.80) render at full opacity. This gives users a visual signal about match quality.

3. **Sort by priority, then confidence.** `ResolverIconRow` sorts icons by user-configured resolver priority first (left-to-right), then by confidence descending within the same priority tier. The icon that appears first is the one that will actually play.

4. **Pass confidences through the full chain.** Every path from resolution to UI must carry confidence scores: `ResolvedSource.confidence` → `RecommendedTrack.resolverConfidences` (or `TrackResolverCache.trackResolverConfidences`) → `TrackRow(resolverConfidences=...)` → `ResolverIconRow(confidences=...)` → `ResolverIconSquare(confidence=...)`.

**Example:** "Clover" by Tundra 212 — streaming resolvers return wrong-song results (0.50 confidence, filtered out). Local files has an exact match (0.95 confidence, shown at full opacity). The user sees only the local files icon and tapping plays the correct local file.

### MBID Enrichment Pipeline

The **ListenBrainz MBID Mapper** (`mapper.listenbrainz.org/mapping/lookup`) resolves artist+title pairs to MusicBrainz identifiers (recording, artist, release MBIDs) in ~4ms with no strict rate limits. `MbidEnrichmentService` manages this with a 90-day TTL disk cache and in-flight deduplication.

**MBID enrichment is wired into every track entry point:**
1. **Library import** — `LibraryRepository.addTrack()`/`addTracks()` calls `enrichBatchInBackground()`
2. **Local file scan** — `MediaScanner.scan()` → `repository.addScannedLocalTracks()` (MBID enrichment as above; see "Local files are the library, not loves")
3. **Background resolution** — `TrackResolverCache.resolveInBackground()` fires MBID enrichment in parallel with resolver resolution
4. **Queue addition** — `PlaybackController.addToQueue()`/`insertNext()` enriches queued tracks
5. **Playback start** — `PlaybackController.playTrackInternal()` enriches the current track
6. **Scrobble dispatch** — `ScrobbleManager` re-reads the track from Room before scrobbling to pick up backfilled MBIDs

**Canonical name fallback:** The mapper returns `artist_credit_name` and `recording_name` — the canonical MusicBrainz names. These are cached in `MbidCacheEntry` and used by `ScrobbleManager` to correct misspelled artist/track names in scrobble payloads (e.g., "Beatles" → "The Beatles").

**Scrobble payloads include MBIDs:** ListenBrainz gets `recording_mbid`, `artist_mbids`, `release_mbid` in both `track_metadata` and `additional_info`. Last.fm and Libre.fm get the `mbid` parameter (recording MBID).

### ISRC → Recording-MBID Fallback (mapper-outage resilience)

The ListenBrainz mapper is the **only** MBID source for un-enriched tracks, and it goes down: `mapper.listenbrainz.org` returned `502` for everyone for 2+ days (June 2026), confirmed even for Radiohead/Creep. During an outage, a freshly-played track never resolves its recording MBID, so the Achordion streaming-link submit on scrobble (#215) — which is recording-keyed — silently skips. The fallback resolves the MBID from the track's **ISRC** via MusicBrainz `GET /ws/2/isrc/{isrc}` (a different service, independently healthy during the mapper outage; exact, ~0.4s). Design doc: `docs/plans/2026-06-16-isrc-mbid-fallback.md`. Desktop parity tracked at [parachord#887](https://github.com/Parachord/parachord/issues/887).

**Service-agnostic by design — the MBID core never branches on a streaming service.** Three layers; adding a new streaming resolver = populate ONE field, nothing downstream changes:

1. **SUPPLY — `ResolvedSource.isrc`, populated per-resolver during resolution.** Each resolver fills it from the response it already fetched, so capture is free (no extra round-trip): native Spotify (`SpTrack.externalIds.isrc` — from the search full-track object → `SpotifyClient.searchTrack` AND the cached-ID **verify** path `ResolverManager.verifySpotifyTrack` via `getTrack`; the verify path is the common case for known-`spotifyId` tracks, so capturing there is what feeds the fallback for most Spotify plays), native Apple Music (catalog `attributes.isrc` — the shared `AppleMusicCatalogResolver` both platforms use; Android's iTunes-Search fallback has no ISRC → null), and the **`.axe` result contract** (an `.axe` resolver MAY return `isrc`; `AxeResolveResult.isrc` → `ResolvedSource.isrc` in both platforms' `resolveAxe`). SoundCloud has no ISRC (null, expected). **Android local files DO carry ISRC** (#238): `MediaScanner` reads the ID3 `TSRC` frame via Media3's metadata extractor (`MetadataRetriever` + `TextInformationFrame` — no new dependency; media3-exoplayer is already on the classpath) at scan time and persists it to the **`tracks.isrc` DB column** — the one place ISRC IS persisted, because it's intrinsic to the file (read once at scan), unlike streaming ISRC which stays in-memory and is re-attached each play. `resolveLocalFile` surfaces it onto the `localfiles` source. ID3-centric on mobile (Vorbis/FLAC/MP4 would need a broader tag lib); iOS has no local-file scanner yet, so its `tracks.isrc` stays null. **Every capture site runs the raw value through `validateIsrc()`** (`ResolverModels.kt` — trim + uppercase + canonical `^[A-Z]{2}[A-Z0-9]{3}[0-9]{7}$`), so a malformed ISRC never enters a source record (desktop drops rather than stores; parachord#894).
2. **CARRY — `Track.isrc`, merged onto the playing track.** Picked via the shared `pickTrackIsrc(topLevelIsrc, sources)` (`ResolverModels.kt`, byte-equivalent with desktop's `window.pickTrackIsrc`): carried top-level first, then walk the resolved sources skipping `noMatch`, each candidate `validateIsrc`-normalized. Used by iOS `IosContainer.trackWithResolvedSources` and Android `PlaybackController.reselectBestSource` **and** `resolveOnTheFly`. **ISRC is NOT a DB column** — it lives only on the live, resolver-merged in-memory track, re-attached each play. Load-bearing consequence: `ScrobbleManager.refreshTrackMbids` re-reads the track from the DB to pick up backfilled MBIDs, which would drop the ISRC; it re-attaches `track.isrc` after the re-read, or the fallback dies for every in-DB track (the common case). Don't remove that re-attach.
3. **CONSUME — service-agnostic core.** `MusicBrainzClient.lookupRecordingMbidByIsrc(isrc)` is pure `isrc → MBID` (returns `recordings.firstOrNull()?.id`; an ISRC is an exact recording identifier, so first-result is safe — no fuzzy wrong-variant risk that a title/artist MB *search* would carry). `MbidEnrichmentService.getRecordingMbid(artist, title, isrc?)` falls back to it **only when the mapper returns null**, and **deliberately does NOT cache the ISRC-only result** — it lacks canonical names + artist/release MBIDs, so caching it (90-day TTL) would block the richer mapper entry once the mapper recovers. The scrobble submit only needs the bare MBID for that play; Achordion dedups by MBID per session. Threaded from `ScrobbleManager.refreshTrackMbids` + `ListenBrainzScrobbler`; `musicBrainzClient` is a nullable ctor param (the fallback is inert without it).

**Key files:** `shared/.../resolver/ResolverModels.kt` (`ResolvedSource.isrc`), `shared/.../model/Track.kt` (`Track.isrc`), `shared/.../api/MusicBrainzClient.kt` (`lookupRecordingMbidByIsrc`), `shared/.../api/SpotifyClient.kt` (`SpExternalIds`), `shared/.../metadata/MbidEnrichmentService.kt` (fallback gate), `app/.../resolver/AndroidResolverRuntime.kt` + `shared/iosMain/.../IosResolverRuntime.kt` (Apple + `.axe` supply), `app/.../playback/PlaybackController.kt` (`reselectBestSource`/`resolveOnTheFly` carry).

### Mapper → acr-lookup fallback, and "unreachable" is not a miss (#394)

`mapper.listenbrainz.org` has been unreachable since 2026-09-21 (a `*.musicbrainz.org` cert that doesn't cover its own hostname; 502 even with verification skipped — LB-2007, open since June). The ISRC fallback above only helps tracks that CARRY an ISRC; Bandcamp/SoundCloud/YouTube/local-without-TSRC tracks lost MBID enrichment entirely. Desktop parachord#991 (`mbid-lookup.js`) is the reference; mobile mirrors it in `ListenBrainzClient.resolveRecordingMbid`:

1. **Mapper first, labs `acr-lookup` only when the mapper can't be reached** (network/TLS error or non-2xx). A mapper `200` with no match is a plain miss — labs is NOT asked. `https://labs.api.listenbrainz.org/acr-lookup/json` returns the same identifiers (`artist_mbids` ≙ the mapper's `artist_credit_mbids`), reported in the SAME `MbidMapperResult` shape with a fixed `confidence = 0.9` and `source = "acr-lookup"`, so every consumer works unchanged. It is a semi-exact matcher (no `(Remastered)`-style suffix tolerance), hence the lower trust below. Labs calls go through a `RateLimitGate` (2 concurrent, 150 ms gap) — the mapper's ~4 ms latency let batch enrichment fire everything at once, and the dataset hoster is a small, strained service.
2. **Three outcomes, never two.** `MbidLookupOutcome` is `Hit` / `Miss(source)` / `Unreachable(errors)`. The old `mbidMapperLookup(): MbidMapperResult?` returned `null` for BOTH "no match" and "request failed", so for the whole outage every failed request was cached as a 90-day miss. **`Unreachable` is never cached and never counted against a track.** `mbidMapperLookup` survives only as a compat wrapper that collapses both to `null` — never feed its `null` into a negative cache.
3. **Per-source miss TTL in `MbidEnrichmentService`:** hit and mapper-miss → 90 days; **acr-lookup miss → 6 hours** (a mapper that comes back may still match); unreachable → not written. `MbidCacheEntry.source` decides the TTL, and its absence is how the **one-shot purge** recognises outage artifacts: on load, a source-less miss with `cachedAt ≥ 2026-09-21` is dropped (`OUTAGE_START_MS`); new misses always carry a source, so it can't re-fire.
4. **The hydration negative cache must not see an outage either.** `HydrationCoordinator` stamps an attempt (7→30-day cooldown) on every `null`/exception from `searchForTrackId`. `ListenBrainzSyncProvider.searchForTrackId` now uses the three-outcome `lookupRecordingMbid` and, when the MBID services were unreachable AND the fuzzy MusicBrainz fallback also found nothing, **throws `TrackIdLookupUnreachableException`** — the one exception the coordinator catches WITHOUT stamping, leaving the track pending for the next cycle. Any other failure still counts as a miss, so broken providers keep backing off. No DB purge of outage-era `track_provider_id_cache` rows was done: first-miss rows (7 d) had already expired by the time this landed, and the fuzzy-MB fallback (MB stayed up) resolved most LB tracks during the outage anyway.

**Key files:** `shared/.../api/ListenBrainzClient.kt` (`resolveRecordingMbid`, `labsGate`, `AcrLookupRowWire`), `shared/.../api/ListenBrainzModels.kt` (`MbidLookupOutcome`, `MbidLookupSource`), `shared/.../metadata/MbidEnrichmentService.kt` (`lookupRecordingMbid`, `RecordingMbidLookup`, `ttlMs`, `isOutageArtifact`), `shared/.../sync/ListenBrainzSyncProvider.kt` (`searchForTrackId`), `shared/.../sync/{HydrationCoordinator,TrackIdLookupUnreachableException}.kt`; tests `ListenBrainzMbidLookupTest` (shared, MockEngine routed by host — a port of desktop's `mbid-lookup.test.js`), `MbidEnrichmentServiceTest`, `ListenBrainzSyncProviderTest`, `HydrationCoordinatorTest`.

### Local File Artwork Validation

`MediaScanner` assigns `content://media/external/audio/albumart/{albumId}` URIs as artwork, but many local files lack embedded art — the URI string exists but points to nothing.

**Two-layer validation:**
1. **Scan time** — `MediaScanner.validateContentUri()` opens the content URI; if it has no data, `artworkUrl` is set to `null` so `ImageEnrichmentService` can fetch art from Last.fm/MusicBrainz.
2. **Playback time** — `PlaybackController.enrichArtworkIfMissing()` revalidates `content://` albumart URIs on the IO thread. If the URI is broken (pre-fix scans or stale data), it falls through to online enrichment.

This means local files without embedded art get artwork pulled from metadata providers, matching how streaming-resolved tracks get their art.

### Playlist Mosaics — Follow the Tracks

A playlist's cover is a 2x2 mosaic of its first four distinct track covers (`PlaylistMosaicPaths.covers`; one cover is used as-is), composed by the platform (`composeMosaicAndroid`, iOS `IosMosaicComposer`) and stored in `playlists.artworkUrl` as a local file URL: `file:/…` on Android (`File.toURI()`), `file:///…` on iOS. Both forms count as a local mosaic everywhere (`PlaylistMosaicPaths.isLocalFile`); a check that only accepts `file://` makes every Android mosaic look missing.

- **The URL carries a fingerprint of its covers**, `?v=<coverSignature>` (FNV-1a, same on every platform). `ImageEnrichmentService.refreshPlaylistMosaic` compares it with the playlist's covers now, from the database only, and rebuilds only on a mismatch. A new `v` also makes Coil and iOS's `PCImageStore` (keyed on the URL) load the rewritten file.
- **When it runs:** at launch for every playlist (`regenerateAllPlaylistMosaics`), and after any write to a playlist's tracks while the app runs: `PlaylistTrackDao.changes` (sync pulls, edits, reorders, hosted XSPF updates, cover backfills) → `watchTrackChanges`, 2 s after that playlist's last write. Desktop builds its 2x2 from the current tracks each session and drops it on save (`getPlaylistCovers`); this is the stored-file equivalent.
- **Stock provider art is replaced** by the mosaic (Spotify's `mosaic.scdn.co`, Apple Music's `AM.PDCXS11.jpg`). A rebuild is stored with a compare-and-set (`replaceArtworkById`) against the art the check saw, so a cover written meanwhile is never overwritten. `updateArtworkById` only writes over blank art, so it can't replace anything. A Spotify pull keeps a local mosaic (`SyncEngine.preserveLocalMosaic`).
- **iOS container moves.** iOS can move the app's data container, which leaves a stored absolute path pointing at nothing.
